<a id='question-147'></a>

### Question 147: Alkaptonuria
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Alkaptonuria is an autosomal recessive disorder caused by a deficiency in homogentisate 1,2-dioxygenase (HGD), leading to the accumulation of homogentisic acid (HGA), which causes ochronosis (darkening of connective tissues) and arthritis. The diagnosis is confirmed by detecting HGA in urine, which darkens upon exposure to air (alkaline oxidation). (卡氏综合征是一种常染色体隐性遗传病，由 homogentisate 1,2-双加氧酶 (HGD) 缺乏引起，导致 homogentisic acid (HGA) 积累，从而引起软骨黑素沉着 (ochronosis) 和关节炎。诊断通过检测尿液中的 HGA 确定，HGA 在暴露于空气后会变暗（碱性氧化）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of severe lower back pain and stiffness, particularly in the morning, which has progressively worsened over the past decade. He denies any history of trauma. His past medical history is unremarkable except for hypertension, well-controlled with lisinopril. Family history reveals that his younger brother also suffered from similar joint problems starting in his 40s. On physical examination, the patient exhibits significant limitation of spinal mobility and crepitus in the lumbar spine. Notably, his ear cartilage appears bluish-black. Urinalysis reveals a dark brown discoloration upon standing for several hours in the air. A spot urine sample is sent for further analysis, which shows elevated levels of homogentisic acid (HGA). Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the HGD gene.

Which of the following biochemical pathways is directly impaired in this patient, leading to the accumulation of homogentisic acid?
(A) Phenylalanine hydroxylase deficiency, impairing tyrosine synthesis.
(B) Homogentisate 1,2-dioxygenase deficiency, impairing the degradation of homogentisic acid.
(C) Fumarate hydratase deficiency, impairing the citric acid cycle.
(D) Tyrosinase deficiency, impairing melanin synthesis.
(E) Galactose-1-phosphate uridyltransferase deficiency, impairing galactose metabolism.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 55-year-old male with progressive lower back pain and stiffness (ochronotic spondylosis), limited spinal mobility, crepitus, bluish-black ear cartilage (ochronosis), and family history suggestive of an inherited disorder. These are classic symptoms of Alkaptonuria.
- **Urinalysis:** Dark brown urine upon standing in air is a hallmark sign of Alkaptonuria, caused by the alkaline oxidation of homogentisic acid (HGA) excreted in the urine.
- **Lab Finding:** Elevated levels of HGA in spot urine confirm the diagnosis.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the HGD gene confirms the underlying genetic defect.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific biochemical pathway impaired, leading to HGA accumulation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Alkaptonuria is an autosomal recessive metabolic disorder.
- The underlying defect is a deficiency in the enzyme homogentisate 1,2-dioxygenase (HGD).
- HGD is encoded by the HGD gene.
- HGD is involved in the catabolism of tyrosine and phenylalanine.
- Specifically, HGD catalyzes the second step in the degradation pathway of homogentisic acid (HGA), converting HGA to maleylacetoacetic acid.
- The reaction requires molecular oxygen (O2) and ferrous iron (Fe2+) as a cofactor.
- Deficiency of HGD leads to the accumulation of HGA in the body.
- HGA is excreted in the urine, causing it to darken upon exposure to air (alkaline oxidation).
- HGA also polymerizes and deposits in connective tissues (cartilage, tendons, ligaments, skin), leading to ochronosis (bluish-black discoloration) and ochronotic arthropathy (degenerative joint disease, especially affecting the spine and large joints).
- The clinical presentation (ochronotic spondylosis, ear cartilage discoloration, family history) and biochemical findings (dark urine, elevated HGA) are consistent with HGD deficiency.
- Therefore, the impaired pathway is the degradation of homogentisic acid by homogentisate 1,2-dioxygenase.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Phenylalanine hydroxylase deficiency causes Phenylketonuria (PKU). This enzyme converts phenylalanine to tyrosine. Deficiency leads to accumulation of phenylalanine and its metabolites (phenylketones), causing intellectual disability, seizures, and musty odor. It does not directly cause HGA accumulation or ochronosis.
- (C) Fumarate hydratase deficiency causes fumarase deficiency, a rare autosomal recessive disorder affecting the citric acid cycle (TCA cycle). It leads to the accumulation of fumarate. Clinical features include neurological problems, developmental delay, and renal tubular acidosis. It is unrelated to tyrosine metabolism or HGA accumulation.
- (D) Tyrosinase deficiency causes Oculocutaneous Albinism Type 1 (OCA1). Tyrosinase is the rate-limiting enzyme in melanin synthesis, converting tyrosine to DOPA and then dopaquinone. Deficiency leads to a lack of melanin pigment, resulting in hypopigmentation of the skin, hair, and eyes. It does not cause HGA accumulation or ochronosis.
- (E) Galactose-1-phosphate uridyltransferase deficiency causes Classic Galactosemia. This enzyme is involved in galactose metabolism. Deficiency leads to the accumulation of galactose-1-phosphate and galactitol, causing liver damage, cataracts, intellectual disability, and failure to thrive in infants upon ingestion of galactose (milk). It is unrelated to tyrosine metabolism or HGA accumulation.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Alkaptonuria is a classic example of an inborn error of metabolism caused by a specific enzyme deficiency (HGD) leading to the accumulation of a metabolic intermediate (HGA) and characteristic clinical features (ochronosis, arthropathy, dark urine). Remember to differentiate it from other disorders of amino acid metabolism like PKU (phenylalanine hydroxylase deficiency) or albinism (tyrosinase deficiency), and disorders affecting other metabolic pathways like galactosemia or TCA cycle defects. The key diagnostic clue is the dark urine that blackens upon standing and the deposition of pigment in connective tissues.

<a id='question-148'></a>

### Question 148: Alkaptonuria
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Alkaptonuria is an autosomal recessive metabolic disorder caused by a deficiency in homogentisate 1,2-dioxygenase (HGD), leading to the accumulation of homogentisic acid (HGA). HGA undergoes oxidation and polymerization, resulting in the deposition of a black pigment (ochronotic pigment) in connective tissues, particularly cartilage, causing ochronosis and severe arthropathy. HGA also causes urine to darken upon standing or exposure to alkali. (Alkaptonuria 是一种常染色体隐性遗传代谢疾病，由 homogentisate 1,2-双加氧酶 (HGD) 缺乏引起，导致 homogentisic acid (HGA) 积累。HGA 氧化和聚合，导致黑色色素（ochronotic pigment）沉积在结缔组织中，特别是软骨中，引起 ochronosis 和严重的关节病。HGA 还导致尿液在静置或暴露于碱性条件下变黑。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of severe lower back pain and stiffness that has progressively worsened over the past 20 years. He reports that his urine turns dark brown or black upon standing or exposure to air, a phenomenon he has noticed since childhood. Physical examination reveals blue-black discoloration of the sclera and ear cartilage. Radiographs of his spine show significant narrowing of the intervertebral disc spaces and osteophyte formation. A urine sample collected and allowed to stand in the air for several hours develops a dark brown color. Laboratory analysis of the patient's urine reveals elevated levels of homogentisic acid (HGA). Enzyme activity assays on cultured fibroblasts show significantly reduced activity of homogentisate 1,2-dioxygenase (HGD). Which of the following biochemical pathways is most directly affected by the underlying defect in this patient?

(A) Phenylalanine metabolism via the tyrosine pathway, specifically the conversion of homogentisate to maleylacetoacetate.
(B) Tryptophan metabolism via the kynurenine pathway, leading to the accumulation of kynurenic acid.
(C) Purine metabolism via the salvage pathway, resulting in the accumulation of uric acid.
(D) Phenylalanine metabolism via the tyrosine pathway, specifically the conversion of tyrosine to p-hydroxyphenylpyruvate.
(E) Methionine metabolism via the transsulfuration pathway, leading to the accumulation of homocysteine.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 55-year-old male with severe, chronic lower back pain and stiffness (arthropathy), blue-black sclera and ear cartilage discoloration (ochronosis), and urine darkening upon standing (alkaptonuria). These are classic clinical hallmarks of Alkaptonuria.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated urinary HGA levels confirm the metabolic defect.
- **Enzyme Assay:** Reduced HGD activity confirms the specific enzyme deficiency responsible for Alkaptonuria.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the biochemical pathway most directly affected by the underlying defect.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Alkaptonuria is caused by mutations in the *HGD* gene, which encodes the enzyme homogentisate 1,2-dioxygenase (also known as homogentisate oxidase).
- This enzyme catalyzes the second step in the degradation pathway of tyrosine and phenylalanine.
- The pathway proceeds as follows: Tyrosine → p-Hydroxyphenylpyruvate → Homogentisate → Maleylacetoacetate → Fumarylacetoacetate → Fumarate + Acetoacetate.
- The HGD enzyme specifically catalyzes the conversion of homogentisate to maleylacetoacetate.
- Deficiency of HGD leads to the accumulation of homogentisate (HGA) in the body.
- HGA is excreted in the urine, where it oxidizes and polymerizes upon exposure to air or alkali, forming a dark pigment, hence the name "alkaptonuria" (alkali + urine).
- HGA also deposits in connective tissues (ochronosis), particularly cartilage, leading to arthritis (arthropathy), especially affecting the spine and large joints.
- Therefore, the biochemical pathway most directly affected is the degradation of tyrosine/phenylalanine, specifically the step catalyzed by HGD, which is the conversion of homogentisate to maleylacetoacetate.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** This option accurately describes the specific step in the tyrosine degradation pathway affected by HGD deficiency in Alkaptonuria. The accumulation of homogentisate is the direct consequence of this blocked step.
- (B): Tryptophan metabolism via the kynurenine pathway is involved in the synthesis of niacin and other metabolites. Defects here lead to conditions like Hartnup disease (impaired tryptophan absorption) or disorders of kynurenine metabolism, not Alkaptonuria. Kynurenic acid accumulation is not characteristic of Alkaptonuria.
- (C): Purine metabolism involves the breakdown of adenine and guanine. Defects in the salvage pathway (e.g., HGPRT deficiency in Lesch-Nyhan syndrome) or de novo synthesis can lead to hyperuricemia and gout. This pathway is unrelated to Alkaptonuria.
- (D): This option describes the first step in the tyrosine degradation pathway, catalyzed by tyrosine aminotransferase. While tyrosine is the precursor, the *specific* step blocked in Alkaptonuria is the conversion of homogentisate, not tyrosine itself. A defect here would affect the initial steps of tyrosine breakdown, not cause HGA accumulation.
- (E): Methionine metabolism involves the transsulfuration pathway (methionine → homocysteine → cysteine) and the remethylation pathway (homocysteine → methionine). Defects can lead to hyperhomocysteinemia, associated with cardiovascular disease and neurological problems. This pathway is unrelated to Alkaptonuria.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Alkaptonuria is a classic example of an inborn error of metabolism affecting the tyrosine degradation pathway. Key features include dark urine on standing, ochronosis (blue-black pigment in cartilage/sclera), and severe arthropathy. The underlying defect is HGD deficiency, blocking the conversion of homogentisate to maleylacetoacetate. Differential diagnoses for dark urine include melaninuria, porphyrins, and certain medications. Differential diagnoses for ochronosis and arthropathy include osteoarthritis and rheumatoid arthritis, but the specific pigment deposition and urine findings point strongly to Alkaptonuria.

<a id='question-149'></a>

### Question 149: Tyrosinemia Type 1
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Nitisinone (NTBC) is a competitive inhibitor of 4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate dioxygenase (HPPD), used to treat hereditary tyrosinemia type 1 (HT1) by preventing the accumulation of toxic metabolites like homogentisic acid and maleylacetoacetic acid. (Nitisinone (NTBC) 是一种 4-羟基苯丙酮酸双加氧酶 (HPPD) 的竞争性抑制剂，用于治疗遗传性 I 型酪氨酸血症 (HT1)，通过防止有毒代谢物如 homogentisic acid 和 maleylacetoacetic acid 的积累。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old infant presents to the emergency department with worsening jaundice, hepatomegaly, and failure to thrive. The infant was born full-term without complications but has shown poor weight gain since birth. Physical examination reveals scleral icterus, an enlarged liver palpable 5 cm below the right costal margin, and ascites. Laboratory investigations show elevated liver enzymes (AST 500 U/L, ALT 450 U/L), hyperbilirubinemia (total bilirubin 8 mg/dL), hypoalbuminemia (albumin 2.5 g/dL), and prolonged prothrombin time (PT 25 seconds). Urine analysis reveals elevated levels of succinylacetone. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *FAH* gene. The infant is started on a specific medication, and follow-up liver biopsy one year later shows significant improvement in liver histology, with decreased fibrosis and reduced steatosis. The medication works by inhibiting the enzyme responsible for converting 4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate to homogentisic acid.

Which of the following biochemical pathways is directly inhibited by the medication used to treat this infant?
(A) Phenylalanine hydroxylase pathway
(B) Homogentisate 1,2-dioxygenase pathway
(C) Fumarylacetoacetate hydrolase pathway
(D) Tyrosine aminotransferase pathway
(E) 4-Hydroxyphenylpyruvate dioxygenase pathway

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 6-month-old infant with jaundice, hepatomegaly, failure to thrive, ascites, elevated liver enzymes, hyperbilirubinemia, hypoalbuminemia, prolonged PT – suggestive of severe liver dysfunction/failure.
- **Urine Analysis:** Elevated succinylacetone – a key diagnostic marker for Hereditary Tyrosinemia Type 1 (HT1). Succinylacetone accumulates due to the block in the tyrosine degradation pathway.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous *FAH* gene mutation – confirms the diagnosis of HT1, caused by deficiency of fumarylacetoacetate hydrolase (FAH).
- **Treatment:** Medication leads to improvement in liver histology – indicates the medication is effective in managing the underlying metabolic defect.
- **Mechanism of Medication:** Inhibits the enzyme converting 4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate to homogentisic acid. This points directly to the enzyme 4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate dioxygenase (HPPD).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the biochemical pathway directly inhibited by the medication.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- The clinical presentation (liver failure, jaundice, ascites) and elevated succinylacetone in urine strongly suggest Hereditary Tyrosinemia Type 1 (HT1).
- HT1 is caused by a deficiency in the enzyme fumarylacetoacetate hydrolase (FAH), encoded by the *FAH* gene. This enzyme catalyzes the final step in the tyrosine degradation pathway, converting fumarylacetoacetate (FAA) to maleylacetoacetate (MAA) and then to acetoacetate.
- Deficiency of FAH leads to the accumulation of its upstream substrates: 4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate (HPP), homogentisic acid (HGA), maleylacetoacetate (MAA), and fumarylacetoacetate (FAA). Succinylacetone is formed non-enzymatically from the condensation of two molecules of FAA.
- These accumulated metabolites, particularly HGA and MAA, are toxic, especially to the liver and kidneys, causing the observed symptoms.
- Nitisinone (NTBC) is the standard treatment for HT1. It is a competitive inhibitor of 4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate dioxygenase (HPPD).
- HPPD catalyzes the conversion of 4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate (HPP) to homogentisic acid (HGA). This step occurs *before* the FAH enzyme deficiency in the tyrosine degradation pathway.
- By inhibiting HPPD, nitisinone prevents the formation of HGA and subsequent toxic metabolites (MAA, FAA, succinylacetone). This reduces the toxic burden on the liver and kidneys, allowing liver function to improve and preventing long-term complications.
- Therefore, the medication directly inhibits the 4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate dioxygenase pathway.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Phenylalanine hydroxylase pathway: This pathway converts phenylalanine to tyrosine. Deficiency causes Phenylketonuria (PKU). Nitisinone does not inhibit this pathway. The key enzyme is phenylalanine hydroxylase (PAH), which requires tetrahydrobiopterin (BH4) as a cofactor.
- (B) Homogentisate 1,2-dioxygenase pathway: This enzyme (also known as homogentisate oxidase) catalyzes the conversion of homogentisic acid to maleylacetoacetate and acetoacetate. Deficiency causes Alkaptonuria. Nitisinone does not inhibit this pathway; it prevents the *formation* of homogentisic acid.
- (C) Fumarylacetoacetate hydrolase pathway: This is the enzyme deficient in HT1 (*FAH* gene). Nitisinone does not inhibit this enzyme; it acts upstream in the pathway. The medication is used *because* this enzyme is deficient.
- (D) Tyrosine aminotransferase pathway: This enzyme (TAT) catalyzes the reversible transamination of tyrosine to 4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate. It is the first step in tyrosine catabolism. Nitisinone does not inhibit this pathway.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Nitisinone (NTBC) is a crucial medication for managing Hereditary Tyrosinemia Type 1 (HT1) by inhibiting 4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate dioxygenase (HPPD), thereby preventing the accumulation of toxic metabolites upstream of the deficient fumarylacetoacetate hydrolase (FAH) enzyme. Key differentials include Alkaptonuria (homogentisate oxidase deficiency) and Phenylketonuria (phenylalanine hydroxylase deficiency).

<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Tyrosinemia Type I
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Defect in tyrosine catabolism leading to accumulation of toxic metabolites, specifically succinylacetone, causing liver and kidney damage, and characteristic skeletal abnormalities. (酪氨酸代谢缺陷导致有毒代谢物琥珀酰乙酸乙酯积累，引起肝脏和肾脏损伤，以及特征性骨骼异常。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant presents to the emergency department with worsening jaundice, hepatomegaly, and failure to thrive. His parents report frequent vomiting and irritability over the past few weeks. Family history is significant for a sibling who died in infancy from unexplained liver failure. Physical examination reveals severe jaundice, an enlarged liver extending 8 cm below the costal margin, and signs of rickets including bowed legs and rachitic rosary. Laboratory investigations show elevated serum transaminases (AST 850 U/L, ALT 700 U/L), hyperbilirubinemia (total bilirubin 12 mg/dL), hypoalbuminemia (albumin 2.0 g/dL), prolonged prothrombin time (PT 25 seconds), and elevated serum creatinine (1.8 mg/dL). Urine analysis reveals the presence of succinylacetone (45 mg/dL, normal < 1 mg/dL) and abnormal amino acids. Genetic testing confirms compound heterozygosity for two missense mutations in the *FAH* gene.

Which of the following biochemical pathways is most directly disrupted in this patient, leading to the accumulation of succinylacetone?
(A) Phenylalanine degradation pathway
(B) Tyrosine degradation pathway
(C) Tryptophan degradation pathway
(D) Histidine degradation pathway
(E) Methionine degradation pathway

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age and Presentation:** 6-month-old infant with jaundice, hepatomegaly, failure to thrive, vomiting, irritability. This points towards a severe metabolic disorder affecting the liver, often presenting in infancy.
- **Family History:** Sibling death from unexplained liver failure suggests an inherited metabolic disorder, likely autosomal recessive given the pattern.
- **Physical Exam:** Jaundice, hepatomegaly, rickets (bowed legs, rachitic rosary). Liver involvement is clear. Rickets suggests impaired vitamin D metabolism or phosphate handling, often seen in proximal renal tubular dysfunction secondary to metabolic disorders.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated liver enzymes (AST, ALT), hyperbilirubinemia, hypoalbuminemia, prolonged PT indicate severe liver dysfunction/failure. Elevated creatinine suggests kidney involvement.
- **Urine Analysis:** Presence of succinylacetone is the key diagnostic clue. This specific metabolite is characteristic of Tyrosinemia Type I. Abnormal amino acids are also expected due to the underlying metabolic defect.
- **Genetic Testing:** Compound heterozygosity in the *FAH* gene confirms the diagnosis of Tyrosinemia Type I. The *FAH* gene encodes fumarylacetoacetate hydrolase.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the biochemical pathway disrupted, leading to succinylacetone accumulation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Tyrosinemia Type I (also known as hepatorenal tyrosinemia) is an autosomal recessive disorder caused by a deficiency in the enzyme fumarylacetoacetate hydrolase (FAH). This enzyme catalyzes the final step in the tyrosine catabolic pathway, converting fumarylacetoacetate (FAA) to fumarate and acetoacetate. The *FAH* gene provides instructions for making this enzyme. Mutations in *FAH* lead to reduced or absent enzyme activity.

In the absence of functional FAH, its substrate, fumarylacetoacetate (FAA), accumulates. FAA is unstable and spontaneously converts to succinylacetone (SA). Succinylacetone is highly toxic, particularly to the liver and kidneys. It inhibits several enzymes, including those involved in heme synthesis (leading to anemia) and the mitochondrial respiratory chain. It also causes proximal renal tubular dysfunction, leading to phosphaturia, aminoaciduria, glycosuria, and impaired vitamin D activation (contributing to rickets).

The tyrosine degradation pathway proceeds as follows:
Tyrosine → 4-Hydroxyphenylpyruvate → Homogentisate → Maleylacetoacetate → Fumarylacetoacetate → Fumarate + Acetoacetate (catalyzed by FAH).

Therefore, the deficiency in FAH directly disrupts the tyrosine degradation pathway, leading to the accumulation of upstream metabolites, most notably the toxic succinylacetone.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Phenylalanine degradation pathway: Phenylalanine is degraded to tyrosine. Defects in this pathway (e.g., Phenylketonuria - PKU, caused by phenylalanine hydroxylase deficiency) lead to accumulation of phenylalanine and its metabolites (phenylketones), causing neurological damage. This pathway does not involve succinylacetone accumulation.
- (C) Tryptophan degradation pathway: Tryptophan is degraded via multiple pathways, including the kynurenine pathway. Defects can lead to conditions like Hartnup disease (transport defect) or carcinoid syndrome (overproduction of serotonin). This pathway does not involve succinylacetone.
- (D) Histidine degradation pathway: Histidine is degraded to glutamate. Defects are rare but can cause histidinemia. This pathway does not involve succinylacetone.
- (E) Methionine degradation pathway: Methionine is degraded via S-adenosylmethionine (SAM) and S-adenosylhomocysteine (SAH) to cysteine. Defects can lead to homocystinuria (e.g., cystathionine beta-synthase deficiency). This pathway does not involve succinylacetone.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Tyrosinemia Type I is caused by FAH deficiency, leading to succinylacetone accumulation. Key features include liver failure, renal tubular dysfunction (causing rickets), and neurological crises. The diagnosis is confirmed by detecting succinylacetone in urine. It is crucial to differentiate it from other metabolic disorders presenting with liver disease, such as galactosemia, hereditary fructose intolerance, or Wilson disease, although the presence of succinylacetone is pathognomonic. The underlying defect is in the tyrosine degradation pathway.
